I wouldn't exactly call it sad. It's more like... just this gray, flat feeling most days. Has been for a long time now. It's like even the things I used to really look forward to just feel like items on a checklist instead of something I actually enjoy. Like fishing, or tending to my tomatoes.

Flooded the raised beds I built out back. I used cedar for those beds, you know, because it doesn't rot as fast, but man, the price of lumber lately is just ridiculous. I went down to the hardware store and couldn't believe what they were charging for a simple two-by-four. It's highway robbery. 

But anyway, yeah, the garden, or even going to a church concert, it’s just... an obligation now. I used to love the music, but now I just sit there listening to the choir and I'm just waiting for it to be over so I can cross it off my list for the day.

Anxious? No, not really. I don't get worked up about things like that. I'm actually a pretty calm guy, usually. Even when the servers went down last Tuesday—total crash, right in the middle of payroll processing. You should have seen the HR director's face. She came storming into my office, waving her arms around, practically hyperventilating about direct deposits. I just sat there, drank my coffee, and told her we had backups running.

Like fishing, or tending to my tomatoes. \n\nSpeaking of which, the tomatoes were an absolute disaster this year. I planted those Early Girls, thinking they'd beat the heat, but we got all that weird rain in June. Flooded the raised beds I built out back. I used cedar for those beds, you know, because it doesn't rot as fast, but man, the price of lumber lately is just ridiculous. I went down to the hardware store and couldn't believe what they were charging for a simple two-by-four. It's highway robbery. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, the garden, or even going to a church concert, it’s just... an obligation now. I used to love the music, but now I just sit there listening to the choir and I'm just waiting for it to be over so I can cross it off my list for the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: Anxious? No, not really. I don't get worked up about things like that. I'm actually a pretty calm guy, usually. Even when the servers went down last Tuesday—total crash, right in the middle of payroll processing. You should have seen the HR director's face. She came storming into my office, waving her arms around, practically hyperventilating about direct deposits. I just sat there, drank my coffee, and told her we had backups running. \n\nSpeaking of coffee, I had to stop drinking that stuff from the breakroom. They switched to this cheap robusta blend to save a few bucks, and it tastes like burnt tires. I ended up bringing in my own French press. Best decision I ever made. The trick is getting the water temperature just right, around 200 degrees, otherwise you over-extract the grounds and it gets all bitter. My wife bought me this electric kettle with a digital thermostat for our anniversary... thirty-two years we've been married. Can you believe that? Time just flies. \n\nBut no, to answer your question, I don't really feel on edge. Just... empty, I guess. Like I'm just going through the motions.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you still find pleasure in the things you used to enjoy?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 63 / computer_or_information_systems_manager / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Pleasure? Not really. Like I said, it's all just... obligations now. I used to get a real kick out of fixing up old radios. Antique ones, you know? Tube radios from the 30s and 40s. There's something so satisfying about tracking down a replacement vacuum tube and hearing that warm hum when it finally powers up. I actually found this gorgeous 1938 Zenith console at a flea market last summer. The guy didn't know what he had, practically gave it to me for forty bucks. The cabinet was a mess, though. Walnut veneer, chipped all to hell. I spent three weeks stripping it down, sanding it, trying to match the original stain. You have to be so careful with veneer, if you sand too hard you go right through it and ruin the whole piece. I used this special tung oil finish... takes forever to cure, but the shine is incredible. Anyway, I finally got it working, put it in the den, and... nothing. I mean, it sounded great, but I just didn't care. I sat there looking at it and just thought, well, that's done. What's the point?

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Weight? Yeah, I suppose so. My wife actually mentioned the other day that my pants are looking a little loose on me. I haven't been trying to lose weight or anything, but my appetite has just been... off, I guess, more days than not. I'll be sitting at my desk, working on some network migration plan, and completely work right through lunch without even noticing I'm hungry. 

Speaking of the network, we're moving everything over to a cloud-based system next month. It's a massive headache. The vendor promised us this seamless integration, but their API documentation is a joke. It's like it was written by someone who's never actually seen a database before. I had my lead developer on a call with them for three hours on Thursday, just trying to get a straight answer about data encryption protocols. You really have to watch these vendors, they'll sell you the moon and deliver a rock. I remember back in the 90s, when we used to host everything locally on these massive IBM mainframes... you actually knew where your data was. It was sitting in a freezing cold server room right down the hall. 

But anyway, yeah, I just don't have much of an appetite lately. Even when I get home, I just kind of pick at dinner. It just doesn't seem worth the effort to eat.

Guilty? No, not really. I don't think I have anything to feel guilty about, honestly. I just go to work, do my job, come home. I still have my dad's old tackle box in the garage somewhere. It's one of those heavy metal ones, painted this ugly olive green, but they don't make them like that anymore. Reminds me of this old server we had back in the 90s, kept dropping the connection for no reason...\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or down most of the time?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't exactly call it sad. It's more like... just this gray, flat feeling most days. Has been for a long time now. It's like even the things I used to really look forward to just feel like items on a checklist instead of something I actually enjoy. Like fishing, or tending to my tomatoes. \n\nSpeaking of which, the tomatoes were an absolute disaster this year. I planted those Early Girls, thinking they'd beat the heat, but we got all that weird rain in June. Flooded the raised beds I built out back. I used cedar for those beds, you know, because it doesn't rot as fast, but man, the price of lumber lately is just ridiculous. I went down to the hardware store and couldn't believe what they were charging for a simple two-by-four. It's highway robbery. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, the garden, or even going to a church concert, it’s just... an obligation now. I used to love the music, but now I just sit there listening to the choir and I'm just waiting for it to be over so I can cross it off my list for the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: Anxious? No, not really. I don't get worked up about things like that. I'm actually a pretty calm guy, usually. Even when the servers went down last Tuesday—total crash, right in the middle of payroll processing. You should have seen the HR director's face. She came storming into my office, waving her arms around, practically hyperventilating about direct deposits. I just sat there, drank my coffee, and told her we had backups running. \n\nSpeaking of coffee, I had to stop drinking that stuff from the breakroom. They switched to this cheap robusta blend to save a few bucks, and it tastes like burnt tires. I ended up bringing in my own French press. Best decision I ever made. The trick is getting the water temperature just right, around 200 degrees, otherwise you over-extract the grounds and it gets all bitter. My wife bought me this electric kettle with a digital thermostat for our anniversary... thirty-two years we've been married. Can you believe that? Time just flies. \n\nBut no, to answer your question, I don't really feel on edge. Just... empty, I guess. Like I'm just going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? Not really. Like I said, it's all just... obligations now. I used to get a real kick out of fixing up old radios. Antique ones, you know? Tube radios from the 30s and 40s. There's something so satisfying about tracking down a replacement vacuum tube and hearing that warm hum when it finally powers up. I actually found this gorgeous 1938 Zenith console at a flea market last summer. The guy didn't know what he had, practically gave it to me for forty bucks. The cabinet was a mess, though. Walnut veneer, chipped all to hell. I spent three weeks stripping it down, sanding it, trying to match the original stain. You have to be so careful with veneer, if you sand too hard you go right through it and ruin the whole piece. I used this special tung oil finish... takes forever to cure, but the shine is incredible. Anyway, I finally got it working, put it in the den, and... nothing. I mean, it sounded great, but I just didn't care. I sat there looking at it and just thought, well, that's done. What's the point?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Weight? Yeah, I suppose so. My wife actually mentioned the other day that my pants are looking a little loose on me. I haven't been trying to lose weight or anything, but my appetite has just been... off, I guess, more days than not. I'll be sitting at my desk, working on some network migration plan, and completely work right through lunch without even noticing I'm hungry. \n\nSpeaking of the network, we're moving everything over to a cloud-based system next month. It's a massive headache. The vendor promised us this seamless integration, but their API documentation is a joke. It's like it was written by someone who's never actually seen a database before. I had my lead developer on a call with them for three hours on Thursday, just trying to get a straight answer about data encryption protocols. You really have to watch these vendors, they'll sell you the moon and deliver a rock. I remember back in the 90s, when we used to host everything locally on these massive IBM mainframes... you actually knew where your data was. It was sitting in a freezing cold server room right down the hall. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I just don't have much of an appetite lately. Even when I get home, I just kind of pick at dinner. It just doesn't seem worth the effort to eat.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about anything?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, not really. I don't think I have anything to feel guilty about, honestly. I just go to work, do my job, come home. Though, speaking of work, I do feel a little bad for my lead developer, Tom. He's a good kid, sharp as a tack, but he's been pulling seventy-hour weeks trying to get this cloud migration sorted out. I told him he needs to take a weekend off, maybe go fishing or something. \n\nI used to love fishing when I was his age. My dad and I would take this little aluminum boat out on the lake right at dawn. The water would be perfectly still, like glass, and the fog would just be lifting off the surface. We'd use these old jitterbug lures... you cast them out near the lily pads and they make this plop-plop-plop sound as you reel them in. Bass go crazy for it. I still have my dad's old tackle box in the garage somewhere. It's one of those heavy metal ones, painted this ugly olive green, but they don't make them like that anymore. Everything now is cheap plastic that cracks if you drop it. \n\nBut no, to get back to what you were asking... I don't feel guilty. Just tired. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
How long? Well, let me think... it's been a while now. This all really started when they pulled me back into full operational leadership at the company. That was, what, about two years and four months ago? Yeah, roughly that. 

They had this younger guy, Greg, running the department for a bit. He had an MBA from somewhere fancy, but he didn't know the first thing about managing IT infrastructure on the ground. He tried to implement this "agile workflow" system that completely bottlenecked our help desk. I mean, you can't just apply software development frameworks to hardware troubleshooting and expect it to work. If a server goes down, you don't put it in a sprint backlog, you fix it right then. I spent three hours one Tuesday just explaining to him why we couldn't outsource our level-one support to a call center in another time zone. Our users are mostly older—accountants and actuaries who have been with the firm for thirty years. When they call IT, they want to talk to someone who knows they always lock themselves out of their email after a long weekend. 

Anyway, things got so bad under Greg that the VP finally asked me to step back into the main leadership role to clean up the mess. And I did. I fixed it. But ever since then, ever since I took that spot back two years and four months ago... I've just been feeling this way. Like the color is just drained out of everything.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, mostly I came in because I’m just... tired. But it’s a weird kind of tired, you know? It doesn't really make sense. I mean, I sleep fine, and I manage to keep up with everything at work. I'm an IT systems manager, have been for years. Actually, we just upgraded the whole network architecture last month, which was a massive headache. You wouldn't believe the pushback I got from the accounting department about the new security protocols. They always want things to be easier, but easier isn't secure, right? Anyway, despite getting enough sleep and getting through the workday, I just feel like I'm running on a low battery before the day even gets going. It's like my internal power supply is just... failing. Reminds me of this old server we had back in the 90s, kept dropping the connection for no reason...\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or down most of the time?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't exactly call it sad. It's more like... just this gray, flat feeling most days. Has been for a long time now. It's like even the things I used to really look forward to just feel like items on a checklist instead of something I actually enjoy. Like fishing, or tending to my tomatoes. \n\nSpeaking of which, the tomatoes were an absolute disaster this year. I planted those Early Girls, thinking they'd beat the heat, but we got all that weird rain in June. Flooded the raised beds I built out back. I used cedar for those beds, you know, because it doesn't rot as fast, but man, the price of lumber lately is just ridiculous. I went down to the hardware store and couldn't believe what they were charging for a simple two-by-four. It's highway robbery. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, the garden, or even going to a church concert, it’s just... an obligation now. I used to love the music, but now I just sit there listening to the choir and I'm just waiting for it to be over so I can cross it off my list for the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: Anxious? No, not really. I don't get worked up about things like that. I'm actually a pretty calm guy, usually. Even when the servers went down last Tuesday—total crash, right in the middle of payroll processing. You should have seen the HR director's face. She came storming into my office, waving her arms around, practically hyperventilating about direct deposits. I just sat there, drank my coffee, and told her we had backups running. \n\nSpeaking of coffee, I had to stop drinking that stuff from the breakroom. They switched to this cheap robusta blend to save a few bucks, and it tastes like burnt tires. I ended up bringing in my own French press. Best decision I ever made. The trick is getting the water temperature just right, around 200 degrees, otherwise you over-extract the grounds and it gets all bitter. My wife bought me this electric kettle with a digital thermostat for our anniversary... thirty-two years we've been married. Can you believe that? Time just flies. \n\nBut no, to answer your question, I don't really feel on edge. Just... empty, I guess. Like I'm just going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? Not really. Like I said, it's all just... obligations now. I used to get a real kick out of fixing up old radios. Antique ones, you know? Tube radios from the 30s and 40s. There's something so satisfying about tracking down a replacement vacuum tube and hearing that warm hum when it finally powers up. I actually found this gorgeous 1938 Zenith console at a flea market last summer. The guy didn't know what he had, practically gave it to me for forty bucks. The cabinet was a mess, though. Walnut veneer, chipped all to hell. I spent three weeks stripping it down, sanding it, trying to match the original stain. You have to be so careful with veneer, if you sand too hard you go right through it and ruin the whole piece. I used this special tung oil finish... takes forever to cure, but the shine is incredible. Anyway, I finally got it working, put it in the den, and... nothing. I mean, it sounded great, but I just didn't care. I sat there looking at it and just thought, well, that's done. What's the point?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Weight? Yeah, I suppose so. My wife actually mentioned the other day that my pants are looking a little loose on me. I haven't been trying to lose weight or anything, but my appetite has just been... off, I guess, more days than not. I'll be sitting at my desk, working on some network migration plan, and completely work right through lunch without even noticing I'm hungry. \n\nSpeaking of the network, we're moving everything over to a cloud-based system next month. It's a massive headache. The vendor promised us this seamless integration, but their API documentation is a joke. It's like it was written by someone who's never actually seen a database before. I had my lead developer on a call with them for three hours on Thursday, just trying to get a straight answer about data encryption protocols. You really have to watch these vendors, they'll sell you the moon and deliver a rock. I remember back in the 90s, when we used to host everything locally on these massive IBM mainframes... you actually knew where your data was. It was sitting in a freezing cold server room right down the hall. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I just don't have much of an appetite lately. Even when I get home, I just kind of pick at dinner. It just doesn't seem worth the effort to eat.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about anything?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, not really. I don't think I have anything to feel guilty about, honestly. I just go to work, do my job, come home. Though, speaking of work, I do feel a little bad for my lead developer, Tom. He's a good kid, sharp as a tack, but he's been pulling seventy-hour weeks trying to get this cloud migration sorted out. I told him he needs to take a weekend off, maybe go fishing or something. \n\nI used to love fishing when I was his age. My dad and I would take this little aluminum boat out on the lake right at dawn. The water would be perfectly still, like glass, and the fog would just be lifting off the surface. We'd use these old jitterbug lures... you cast them out near the lily pads and they make this plop-plop-plop sound as you reel them in. Bass go crazy for it. I still have my dad's old tackle box in the garage somewhere. It's one of those heavy metal ones, painted this ugly olive green, but they don't make them like that anymore. Everything now is cheap plastic that cracks if you drop it. \n\nBut no, to get back to what you were asking... I don't feel guilty. Just tired. Just sort of... flat.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: How long? Well, let me think... it's been a while now. This all really started when they pulled me back into full operational leadership at the company. That was, what, about two years and four months ago? Yeah, roughly that. \n\nThey had this younger guy, Greg, running the department for a bit. He had an MBA from somewhere fancy, but he didn't know the first thing about managing IT infrastructure on the ground. He tried to implement this \"agile workflow\" system that completely bottlenecked our help desk. I mean, you can't just apply software development frameworks to hardware troubleshooting and expect it to work. If a server goes down, you don't put it in a sprint backlog, you fix it right then. I spent three hours one Tuesday just explaining to him why we couldn't outsource our level-one support to a call center in another time zone. Our users are mostly older—accountants and actuaries who have been with the firm for thirty years. When they call IT, they want to talk to someone who knows they always lock themselves out of their email after a long weekend. \n\nAnyway, things got so bad under Greg that the VP finally asked me to step back into the main leadership role to clean up the mess. And I did. I fixed it. But ever since then, ever since I took that spot back two years and four months ago... I've just been feeling this way. Like the color is just drained out of everything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Like the color is just drained out of everything.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre there any activities that you still enjoy or look forward to?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 63 / computer_or_information_systems_manager / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Depressed_Mood\nmanifestation: \"For a long time now there’s been this gray, flat feeling most days, where even things I used to look forward to like fishing, tending the tomatoes, or a church concert feel like items on a checklist instead of something I enjoy.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Enjoy? Look forward to? Not really, no. For a long time now, there's just been this... this gray, flat feeling most days. It's like a heavy blanket. Even the things I used to really like doing—you know, like I mentioned the fishing, or tending to my tomatoes out back, or even going to one of those choir concerts at church with my wife—they just feel like items on a checklist now. I do them, but there's no joy in it. 

Speaking of the tomatoes, though, the soil quality this year has been absolutely terrible. I think it's because of all that unseasonable rain we got back in April. Washes all the nitrogen right out of the dirt. I was down at the garden center last weekend, trying to find a decent fertilizer, and the kid working there didn't even know the difference between bone meal and blood meal. I swear, they hire these high school kids who spend the whole shift looking at their phones. I asked him where the 10-10-10 mix was, and he looked at me like I was speaking Greek. Back when old Mr. Henderson ran the hardware store on Main Street, he knew exactly what you needed before you even walked in the door. He had this big golden retriever that would sleep right under the cash register... 

But anyway, yeah. I just don't get much enjoyment out of anything anymore. It's all just... going through the motions.

It's like my internal power supply is just... failing. I planted those Early Girls, thinking they'd beat the heat, but we got all that weird rain in June. Flooded the raised beds I built out back. I used cedar for those beds, you know, because it doesn't rot as fast, but man, the price of lumber lately is just ridiculous. I went down to the hardware store and couldn't believe what they were charging for a simple two-by-four. I used to love the music, but now I just sit there listening to the choir and I'm just waiting for it to be over so I can cross it off my list for the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: Anxious? No, not really. I don't get worked up about things like that. I'm actually a pretty calm guy, usually. Even when the servers went down last Tuesday—total crash, right in the middle of payroll processing. You should have seen the HR director's face. She came storming into my office, waving her arms around, practically hyperventilating about direct deposits. I just sat there, drank my coffee, and told her we had backups running. \n\nSpeaking of coffee, I had to stop drinking that stuff from the breakroom. They switched to this cheap robusta blend to save a few bucks, and it tastes like burnt tires. I ended up bringing in my own French press. Best decision I ever made. The trick is getting the water temperature just right, around 200 degrees, otherwise you over-extract the grounds and it gets all bitter. My wife bought me this electric kettle with a digital thermostat for our anniversary... thirty-two years we've been married. Can you believe that? Time just flies. \n\nBut no, to answer your question, I don't really feel on edge. Just... empty, I guess. Like I'm just going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? Not really. Like I said, it's all just... obligations now. I used to get a real kick out of fixing up old radios. Antique ones, you know? Tube radios from the 30s and 40s. There's something so satisfying about tracking down a replacement vacuum tube and hearing that warm hum when it finally powers up. I actually found this gorgeous 1938 Zenith console at a flea market last summer. The guy didn't know what he had, practically gave it to me for forty bucks. The cabinet was a mess, though. Walnut veneer, chipped all to hell. I spent three weeks stripping it down, sanding it, trying to match the original stain. You have to be so careful with veneer, if you sand too hard you go right through it and ruin the whole piece. I used this special tung oil finish... takes forever to cure, but the shine is incredible. Anyway, I finally got it working, put it in the den, and... nothing. I mean, it sounded great, but I just didn't care. I sat there looking at it and just thought, well, that's done. What's the point?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Weight? Yeah, I suppose so. My wife actually mentioned the other day that my pants are looking a little loose on me. I haven't been trying to lose weight or anything, but my appetite has just been... off, I guess, more days than not. I'll be sitting at my desk, working on some network migration plan, and completely work right through lunch without even noticing I'm hungry. \n\nSpeaking of the network, we're moving everything over to a cloud-based system next month. It's a massive headache. The vendor promised us this seamless integration, but their API documentation is a joke. It's like it was written by someone who's never actually seen a database before. I had my lead developer on a call with them for three hours on Thursday, just trying to get a straight answer about data encryption protocols. You really have to watch these vendors, they'll sell you the moon and deliver a rock. I remember back in the 90s, when we used to host everything locally on these massive IBM mainframes... you actually knew where your data was. It was sitting in a freezing cold server room right down the hall. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I just don't have much of an appetite lately. Even when I get home, I just kind of pick at dinner. It just doesn't seem worth the effort to eat.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about anything?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, not really. I don't think I have anything to feel guilty about, honestly. I just go to work, do my job, come home. Though, speaking of work, I do feel a little bad for my lead developer, Tom. He's a good kid, sharp as a tack, but he's been pulling seventy-hour weeks trying to get this cloud migration sorted out. I told him he needs to take a weekend off, maybe go fishing or something. \n\nI used to love fishing when I was his age. My dad and I would take this little aluminum boat out on the lake right at dawn. The water would be perfectly still, like glass, and the fog would just be lifting off the surface. We'd use these old jitterbug lures... you cast them out near the lily pads and they make this plop-plop-plop sound as you reel them in. Bass go crazy for it. I still have my dad's old tackle box in the garage somewhere. It's one of those heavy metal ones, painted this ugly olive green, but they don't make them like that anymore. Everything now is cheap plastic that cracks if you drop it. \n\nBut no, to get back to what you were asking... I don't feel guilty. Just tired. Just sort of... flat.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: How long? Well, let me think... it's been a while now. Our users are mostly older—accountants and actuaries who have been with the firm for thirty years. I do them, but there's no joy in it. \n\nSpeaking of the tomatoes, though, the soil quality this year has been absolutely terrible. I think it's because of all that unseasonable rain we got back in April. Washes all the nitrogen right out of the dirt. I was down at the garden center last weekend, trying to find a decent fertilizer, and the kid working there didn't even know the difference between bone meal and blood meal. I swear, they hire these high school kids who spend the whole shift looking at their phones. I asked him where the 10-10-10 mix was, and he looked at me like I was speaking Greek. Back when old Mr. Henderson ran the hardware store on Main Street, he knew exactly what you needed before you even walked in the door. He had this big golden retriever that would sleep right under the cash register... \n\nBut anyway, yeah. I just don't get much enjoyment out of anything anymore. It's all just... going through the motions.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre there any small pleasures in life that you still find enjoyable?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I mean, you can't just apply software development frameworks to hardware troubleshooting and expect it to work. If a server goes down, you don't put it in a sprint backlog, you fix it right then. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Small pleasures? No, not really. I mean, I guess I'd say no. Everything just feels like work, you know? Even eating. My wife made pot roast on Sunday, and usually I love her pot roast. She uses this specific cut of chuck roast and slow cooks it with carrots and those little pearl onions. 

She used to get the onions from the farmer's market downtown, back before they moved it to that new location by the old textile mill. I don't know why they moved it. Parking there is an absolute nightmare. You have to park three blocks away and lug your bags over these cracked sidewalks that the city hasn't fixed since the nineties. I wrote a letter to the city council about it last year. Not that they care. They're too busy approving those ugly modern apartment buildings that look like stacked shipping containers. There's one going up right next to my favorite deli, completely blocking their afternoon sun. The guy who runs the deli, Sal, he's furious about it. He makes the best pastrami on rye in the county, cures the meat himself. 

But, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right. Small pleasures. No, even the pot roast just tasted like... nothing. Just chewing to stay alive, I guess.

Suicide? No. No, I haven't had any thoughts like that. I mean, things feel gray and heavy, sure, but I don't want to die. Plus, I couldn't do that to my wife, especially not with the absolute mess our estate paperwork is in right now. 

We actually just tried to update our life insurance policies a couple of weeks ago. You wouldn't believe the hoops they make you jump through now. The insurance company—Standard Mutual, I think it was—they just migrated to this new online portal, and whoever coded their authentication system should be fired. It completely rejected my two-factor token. I had to sit on hold for forty-five minutes listening to this awful, tinny elevator music. It was a smooth jazz version of "The Girl from Ipanema" on a loop, with just terrible audio compression. I told the rep when she finally picked up that they really need to increase the bit rate on their telephony system, but she had no idea what I was talking about. I could hear a dog barking in the background, so she was probably just working from her kitchen table anyway. It's amazing how these massive financial institutions handle their IT infrastructure these days. Nobody does quality assurance before rolling out a major update. 

But, uh... sorry, what was I saying? Oh, right. No. No thoughts of suicide or anything like that. I'm just... tired of feeling this way.

]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yeah, actually. This whole gray feeling, it really started settling in about two years and four months ago. That was right around the time I got pulled back into full operational leadership at work. 

I was supposed to be transitioning into more of an advisory role, you know? Taking a step back. But then our VP of Engineering decided to jump ship to some startup in Silicon Valley. Typical, right? These guys get dollar signs in their eyes and just abandon a ten-year roadmap. And the worst part is, the startup he went to is trying to build some blockchain-based supply chain solution. I told him before he left, I said, "Blockchain is a solution looking for a problem in that sector. A centralized relational database is faster, cheaper, and easier to audit." But nobody listens to the old IT guys anymore. They just want the buzzwords for their pitch decks. 

So anyway, because he left, they dumped his entire portfolio on my desk. Suddenly I'm managing three different development teams across four time zones. Trying to get the offshore team in Bangalore to sync up with the local QA guys in Chicago is like herding cats. The latency on our daily stand-up calls alone drives me crazy. We use this new video conferencing software the procurement department bought because it was cheaper, and the packet loss is atrocious. Half the time, people look like blocky, pixelated ghosts, and the audio drops out right when they're giving critical status updates. I submitted a business case to upgrade our network infrastructure to handle the bandwidth, but finance rejected it. Said it wasn't in the Q3 budget. 

But, uh... yeah. So it was about two years and four months ago, when all that operational leadership stuff got dropped in my lap. That's when I really started feeling this way.

Like I'm just going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? Not really. Like I said, it's all just... obligations now. There's something so satisfying about tracking down a replacement vacuum tube and hearing that warm hum when it finally powers up. I actually found this gorgeous 1938 Zenith console at a flea market last summer. The guy didn't know what he had, practically gave it to me for forty bucks. The cabinet was a mess, though. Walnut veneer, chipped all to hell. I spent three weeks stripping it down, sanding it, trying to match the original stain. You have to be so careful with veneer, if you sand too hard you go right through it and ruin the whole piece. I used this special tung oil finish... takes forever to cure, but the shine is incredible. Anyway, I finally got it working, put it in the den, and... nothing. I mean, it sounded great, but I just didn't care. I sat there looking at it and just thought, well, that's done. What's the point?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Weight? Yeah, I suppose so. My wife actually mentioned the other day that my pants are looking a little loose on me. I haven't been trying to lose weight or anything, but my appetite has just been... off, I guess, more days than not. I'll be sitting at my desk, working on some network migration plan, and completely work right through lunch without even noticing I'm hungry. \n\nSpeaking of the network, we're moving everything over to a cloud-based system next month. It's a massive headache. The vendor promised us this seamless integration, but their API documentation is a joke. It's like it was written by someone who's never actually seen a database before. I had my lead developer on a call with them for three hours on Thursday, just trying to get a straight answer about data encryption protocols. You really have to watch these vendors, they'll sell you the moon and deliver a rock. I remember back in the 90s, when we used to host everything locally on these massive IBM mainframes... you actually knew where your data was. It was sitting in a freezing cold server room right down the hall. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I just don't have much of an appetite lately. Even when I get home, I just kind of pick at dinner. It just doesn't seem worth the effort to eat.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about anything?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, not really. I don't think I have anything to feel guilty about, honestly. I just go to work, do my job, come home. Though, speaking of work, I do feel a little bad for my lead developer, Tom. He's a good kid, sharp as a tack, but he's been pulling seventy-hour weeks trying to get this cloud migration sorted out. I told him he needs to take a weekend off, maybe go fishing or something. \n\nI used to love fishing when I was his age. My dad and I would take this little aluminum boat out on the lake right at dawn. The water would be perfectly still, like glass, and the fog would just be lifting off the surface. We'd use these old jitterbug lures... you cast them out near the lily pads and they make this plop-plop-plop sound as you reel them in. Bass go crazy for it. I still have my dad's old tackle box in the garage somewhere. It's one of those heavy metal ones, painted this ugly olive green, but they don't make them like that anymore. Everything now is cheap plastic that cracks if you drop it. \n\nBut no, to get back to what you were asking... I don't feel guilty. Just tired. Just sort of... flat.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: How long? Well, let me think... it's been a while now. This all really started when they pulled me back into full operational leadership at the company. That was, what, about two years and four months ago? Yeah, roughly that. \n\nThey had this younger guy, Greg, running the department for a bit. He had an MBA from somewhere fancy, but he didn't know the first thing about managing IT infrastructure on the ground. He tried to implement this \"agile workflow\" system that completely bottlenecked our help desk. I mean, you can't just apply software development frameworks to hardware troubleshooting and expect it to work. If a server goes down, you don't put it in a sprint backlog, you fix it right then. I spent three hours one Tuesday just explaining to him why we couldn't outsource our level-one support to a call center in another time zone. Our users are mostly older—accountants and actuaries who have been with the firm for thirty years. When they call IT, they want to talk to someone who knows they always lock themselves out of their email after a long weekend. \n\nAnyway, things got so bad under Greg that the VP finally asked me to step back into the main leadership role to clean up the mess. And I did. I fixed it. But ever since then, ever since I took that spot back two years and four months ago... I've just been feeling this way. Like the color is just drained out of everything.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Enjoy? Look forward to? Not really, no. For a long time now, there's just been this... this gray, flat feeling most days. It's like a heavy blanket. Even the things I used to really like doing—you know, like I mentioned the fishing, or tending to my tomatoes out back, or even going to one of those choir concerts at church with my wife—they just feel like items on a checklist now. I do them, but there's no joy in it. \n\nSpeaking of the tomatoes, though, the soil quality this year has been absolutely terrible. I think it's because of all that unseasonable rain we got back in April. Washes all the nitrogen right out of the dirt. I was down at the garden center last weekend, trying to find a decent fertilizer, and the kid working there didn't even know the difference between bone meal and blood meal. I swear, they hire these high school kids who spend the whole shift looking at their phones. I asked him where the 10-10-10 mix was, and he looked at me like I was speaking Greek. Back when old Mr. Henderson ran the hardware store on Main Street, he knew exactly what you needed before you even walked in the door. He had this big golden retriever that would sleep right under the cash register... \n\nBut anyway, yeah. I just don't get much enjoyment out of anything anymore. It's all just... going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small pleasures in life that you still find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: Small pleasures? No, not really. I mean, I guess I'd say no. Everything just feels like work, you know? Even eating. My wife made pot roast on Sunday, and usually I love her pot roast. She uses this specific cut of chuck roast and slow cooks it with carrots and those little pearl onions. \n\nShe used to get the onions from the farmer's market downtown, back before they moved it to that new location by the old textile mill. I don't know why they moved it. Parking there is an absolute nightmare. You have to park three blocks away and lug your bags over these cracked sidewalks that the city hasn't fixed since the nineties. I wrote a letter to the city council about it last year. Not that they care. They're too busy approving those ugly modern apartment buildings that look like stacked shipping containers. There's one going up right next to my favorite deli, completely blocking their afternoon sun. The guy who runs the deli, Sal, he's furious about it. He makes the best pastrami on rye in the county, cures the meat himself. \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right. Small pleasures. No, even the pot roast just tasted like... nothing. Just chewing to stay alive, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about not wanting to be alive or had thoughts of suicide?\n\nPatient: Suicide? No. No, I haven't had any thoughts like that. I mean, things feel gray and heavy, sure, but I don't want to die. You wouldn't believe the hoops they make you jump through now. The insurance company—Standard Mutual, I think it was—they just migrated to this new online portal, and whoever coded their authentication system should be fired. It completely rejected my two-factor token. I had to sit on hold for forty-five minutes listening to this awful, tinny elevator music. It was a smooth jazz version of \"The Girl from Ipanema\" on a loop, with just terrible audio compression. I told the rep when she finally picked up that they really need to increase the bit rate on their telephony system, but she had no idea what I was talking about. I could hear a dog barking in the background, so she was probably just working from her kitchen table anyway. It's amazing how these massive financial institutions handle their IT infrastructure these days. Nobody does quality assurance before rolling out a major update. \n\nBut, uh... sorry, what was I saying? Oh, right. No. No thoughts of suicide or anything like that. I'm just... tired of feeling this way.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a significant loss or major change in your life around the time you started feeling this way, about two years and four months ago?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, actually. I told him before he left, I said, \"Blockchain is a solution looking for a problem in that sector. A centralized relational database is faster, cheaper, and easier to audit.\" But nobody listens to the old IT guys anymore. They just want the buzzwords for their pitch decks. \n\nSo anyway, because he left, they dumped his entire portfolio on my desk. Suddenly I'm managing three different development teams across four time zones. Trying to get the offshore team in Bangalore to sync up with the local QA guys in Chicago is like herding cats. The latency on our daily stand-up calls alone drives me crazy. We use this new video conferencing software the procurement department bought because it was cheaper, and the packet loss is atrocious. Half the time, people look like blocky, pixelated ghosts, and the audio drops out right when they're giving critical status updates. I submitted a business case to upgrade our network infrastructure to handle the bandwidth, but finance rejected it. Said it wasn't in the Q3 budget. \n\nBut, uh... yeah. So it was about two years and four months ago, when all that operational leadership stuff got dropped in my lap. That's when I really started feeling this way.\n\nDoctor: Has this gray, flat feeling you've been describing lasted for more than two years?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. It's been going on since I got pulled back into that full operational leadership role, which, like I said, was about two years and four months ago. It feels like a lifetime, though, honestly. \n\nI mean, time just drags when you're stuck in endless meetings about resource allocation. We had this one meeting last week—three hours long—just to argue about whether we should migrate our legacy servers to AWS or Azure. I told them, \"Look, Azure integrates better with our existing Microsoft enterprise licenses,\" but the new CTO—this guy they brought in from some retail conglomerate—he's obsessed with AWS because he read a white paper about their new machine learning instances. As if we even use machine learning! We're processing inventory data for auto parts, for crying out loud. We don't need a neural network to tell us when we're low on brake pads. I spent an entire weekend pulling together a cost-benefit analysis in Excel, complete with pivot tables and projected five-year total cost of ownership, and he didn't even look at it. Just skimmed the executive summary and said, \"Let's put a pin in this.\" Put a pin in it! I hate that corporate speak. \n\nBut, uh, sorry. What was the question? Oh, the timeline. Yeah, over two years now. Two years and four months of this... whatever this is.\n\n[Last Question]\nWas there a specific traumatic event or stressor that occurred around the time you started feeling this way, about two years and four months ago?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 63 / computer_or_information_systems_manager / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, no specific trauma. Just the work stress I mentioned. I mean, getting all those teams dumped on me was stressful, sure, but it wasn't like a car crash or anything. It was just the realization that I was going to be stuck dealing with the day-to-day grind again instead of coasting into retirement. 

Speaking of retirement, my wife and I were actually looking at these properties down in Florida a while back. Not Miami or anything crazy like that, but over on the Gulf Coast. Sarasota area. The property taxes down there are so much better than what we pay here in Illinois. But then you have to factor in the homeowners association fees, which are just exorbitant. We found this one place, beautiful little ranch house, right on a golf course. I don't even golf, but the view was nice. But the HOA rules! They had a fifty-page PDF documenting exactly what color you could paint your mailbox and what type of grass you were allowed to plant. And they required you to submit any landscaping changes through this proprietary web portal that looked like it was built in 1998 using Microsoft FrontPage. I inspected the page source, and it was full of deprecated HTML tags. I couldn't in good conscience live in a community that relies on an unmaintained, unsecure web application to manage its operations. Imagine if their database got breached—all those residents' financial details, just sitting there in plain text, probably. 

So, yeah, we put the Florida idea on hold. But anyway, to answer your question... no. No big trauma. Just the work stuff I told you about.

We'd use these old jitterbug lures... you cast them out near the lily pads and they make this plop-plop-plop sound as you reel them in. Bass go crazy for it. I still have my dad's old tackle box in the garage somewhere. It's one of those heavy metal ones, painted this ugly olive green, but they don't make them like that anymore. Everything now is cheap plastic that cracks if you drop it. \n\nBut no, to get back to what you were asking... I don't feel guilty. Just tired. Just sort of... flat.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: How long? Well, let me think... it's been a while now. This all really started when they pulled me back into full operational leadership at the company. That was, what, about two years and four months ago? Yeah, roughly that. \n\nThey had this younger guy, Greg, running the department for a bit. He had an MBA from somewhere fancy, but he didn't know the first thing about managing IT infrastructure on the ground. He tried to implement this \"agile workflow\" system that completely bottlenecked our help desk. I mean, you can't just apply software development frameworks to hardware troubleshooting and expect it to work. If a server goes down, you don't put it in a sprint backlog, you fix it right then. I spent three hours one Tuesday just explaining to him why we couldn't outsource our level-one support to a call center in another time zone. Our users are mostly older—accountants and actuaries who have been with the firm for thirty years. When they call IT, they want to talk to someone who knows they always lock themselves out of their email after a long weekend. \n\nAnyway, things got so bad under Greg that the VP finally asked me to step back into the main leadership role to clean up the mess. And I did. I fixed it. But ever since then, ever since I took that spot back two years and four months ago... I've just been feeling this way. Like the color is just drained out of everything.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Enjoy? Look forward to? Not really, no. For a long time now, there's just been this... this gray, flat feeling most days. It's like a heavy blanket. Even the things I used to really like doing—you know, like I mentioned the fishing, or tending to my tomatoes out back, or even going to one of those choir concerts at church with my wife—they just feel like items on a checklist now. I do them, but there's no joy in it. \n\nSpeaking of the tomatoes, though, the soil quality this year has been absolutely terrible. I think it's because of all that unseasonable rain we got back in April. Washes all the nitrogen right out of the dirt. I was down at the garden center last weekend, trying to find a decent fertilizer, and the kid working there didn't even know the difference between bone meal and blood meal. I swear, they hire these high school kids who spend the whole shift looking at their phones. I asked him where the 10-10-10 mix was, and he looked at me like I was speaking Greek. Back when old Mr. Henderson ran the hardware store on Main Street, he knew exactly what you needed before you even walked in the door. He had this big golden retriever that would sleep right under the cash register... \n\nBut anyway, yeah. I just don't get much enjoyment out of anything anymore. It's all just... going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small pleasures in life that you still find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: Small pleasures? No, not really. I mean, I guess I'd say no. Everything just feels like work, you know? Even eating. My wife made pot roast on Sunday, and usually I love her pot roast. She uses this specific cut of chuck roast and slow cooks it with carrots and those little pearl onions. \n\nShe used to get the onions from the farmer's market downtown, back before they moved it to that new location by the old textile mill. I don't know why they moved it. Parking there is an absolute nightmare. You have to park three blocks away and lug your bags over these cracked sidewalks that the city hasn't fixed since the nineties. I wrote a letter to the city council about it last year. Not that they care. They're too busy approving those ugly modern apartment buildings that look like stacked shipping containers. There's one going up right next to my favorite deli, completely blocking their afternoon sun. The guy who runs the deli, Sal, he's furious about it. He makes the best pastrami on rye in the county, cures the meat himself. \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right. Small pleasures. No, even the pot roast just tasted like... nothing. Just chewing to stay alive, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about not wanting to be alive or had thoughts of suicide?\n\nPatient: Suicide? No. No, I haven't had any thoughts like that. I mean, things feel gray and heavy, sure, but I don't want to die. Plus, I couldn't do that to my wife, especially not with the absolute mess our estate paperwork is in right now. \n\nWe actually just tried to update our life insurance policies a couple of weeks ago. You wouldn't believe the hoops they make you jump through now. The insurance company—Standard Mutual, I think it was—they just migrated to this new online portal, and whoever coded their authentication system should be fired. It completely rejected my two-factor token. I had to sit on hold for forty-five minutes listening to this awful, tinny elevator music. It was a smooth jazz version of \"The Girl from Ipanema\" on a loop, with just terrible audio compression. I told the rep when she finally picked up that they really need to increase the bit rate on their telephony system, but she had no idea what I was talking about. I could hear a dog barking in the background, so she was probably just working from her kitchen table anyway. It's amazing how these massive financial institutions handle their IT infrastructure these days. Nobody does quality assurance before rolling out a major update. \n\nBut, uh... sorry, what was I saying? Oh, right. No. No thoughts of suicide or anything like that. I'm just... tired of feeling this way.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a significant loss or major change in your life around the time you started feeling this way, about two years and four months ago?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, actually. This whole gray feeling, it really started settling in about two years and four months ago. That was right around the time I got pulled back into full operational leadership at work. \n\nI was supposed to be transitioning into more of an advisory role, you know? Taking a step back. But then our VP of Engineering decided to jump ship to some startup in Silicon Valley. Typical, right? These guys get dollar signs in their eyes and just abandon a ten-year roadmap. And the worst part is, the startup he went to is trying to build some blockchain-based supply chain solution. I told him before he left, I said, \"Blockchain is a solution looking for a problem in that sector. A centralized relational database is faster, cheaper, and easier to audit.\" But nobody listens to the old IT guys anymore. They just want the buzzwords for their pitch decks. \n\nSo anyway, because he left, they dumped his entire portfolio on my desk. Suddenly I'm managing three different development teams across four time zones. Trying to get the offshore team in Bangalore to sync up with the local QA guys in Chicago is like herding cats. The latency on our daily stand-up calls alone drives me crazy. We use this new video conferencing software the procurement department bought because it was cheaper, and the packet loss is atrocious. Half the time, people look like blocky, pixelated ghosts, and the audio drops out right when they're giving critical status updates. I submitted a business case to upgrade our network infrastructure to handle the bandwidth, but finance rejected it. Said it wasn't in the Q3 budget. \n\nBut, uh... yeah. So it was about two years and four months ago, when all that operational leadership stuff got dropped in my lap. That's when I really started feeling this way.\n\nDoctor: Has this gray, flat feeling you've been describing lasted for more than two years?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. It's been going on since I got pulled back into that full operational leadership role, which, like I said, was about two years and four months ago. It feels like a lifetime, though, honestly. \n\nI mean, time just drags when you're stuck in endless meetings about resource allocation. We had this one meeting last week—three hours long—just to argue about whether we should migrate our legacy servers to AWS or Azure. I told them, \"Look, Azure integrates better with our existing Microsoft enterprise licenses,\" but the new CTO—this guy they brought in from some retail conglomerate—he's obsessed with AWS because he read a white paper about their new machine learning instances. As if we even use machine learning! We're processing inventory data for auto parts, for crying out loud. We don't need a neural network to tell us when we're low on brake pads. I spent an entire weekend pulling together a cost-benefit analysis in Excel, complete with pivot tables and projected five-year total cost of ownership, and he didn't even look at it. Just skimmed the executive summary and said, \"Let's put a pin in this.\" Put a pin in it! I hate that corporate speak. \n\nBut, uh, sorry. What was the question? Oh, the timeline. Yeah, over two years now. Two years and four months of this... whatever this is.\n\nDoctor: Was there a specific traumatic event or stressor that occurred around the time you started feeling this way, about two years and four months ago?\n\nPatient: No, no specific trauma. Just the work stress I mentioned. I mean, getting all those teams dumped on me was stressful, sure, but it wasn't like a car crash or anything. It was just the realization that I was going to be stuck dealing with the day-to-day grind again instead of coasting into retirement. \n\nSpeaking of retirement, my wife and I were actually looking at these properties down in Florida a while back. Not Miami or anything crazy like that, but over on the Gulf Coast. Sarasota area. The property taxes down there are so much better than what we pay here in Illinois. But then you have to factor in the homeowners association fees, which are just exorbitant. We found this one place, beautiful little ranch house, right on a golf course. I don't even golf, but the view was nice. But the HOA rules! They had a fifty-page PDF documenting exactly what color you could paint your mailbox and what type of grass you were allowed to plant. And they required you to submit any landscaping changes through this proprietary web portal that looked like it was built in 1998 using Microsoft FrontPage. I inspected the page source, and it was full of deprecated HTML tags. I couldn't in good conscience live in a community that relies on an unmaintained, unsecure web application to manage its operations. Imagine if their database got breached—all those residents' financial details, just sitting there in plain text, probably. \n\nSo, yeah, we put the Florida idea on hold. No, no... nobody died, no divorce, nothing like that. Just that promotion—well, "promotion"—that I didn't want. 

You know, it's funny you ask about losing things, though, because I did lose my favorite coffee mug around that time. I know that sounds stupid, but I'd had it for fifteen years. It was one of those heavy ceramic ones, kept the heat perfectly. I got it at this tech conference in Vegas back in... oh, must have been 2008. The year they announced that massive shift in server architecture. Anyway, I left it in the breakroom after one of those endless AWS versus Azure meetings I told you about. Went back an hour later, and it was gone. Just vanished. I even checked the security camera footage—I have admin access to the system, obviously—but there's a blind spot right by the sink. I submitted a ticket to facilities to have the camera angle adjusted, because if someone can steal a mug, they could steal a laptop, right? It's a glaring security flaw. They closed the ticket without even commenting. Just marked it "Resolved." It wasn't resolved! My mug is still gone, and the blind spot is still there. 

But, uh, as for people or... or important life things? No, nothing like that. Just the mug and my peace of mind, I guess.
